</div></td></tr><tr style="display:none"><td colspan="2">

</td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>John of Damascus</b> (<a href="/wiki/Arabic_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Arabic language">Arabic</a>: <span lang="ar" dir="rtl">يوحنا الدمشقي</span>, <small><a href="/wiki/Romanization_of_Arabic" title="Romanization of Arabic">romanized</a>:&#160;</small><span title="Arabic-language romanization"><i lang="ar-Latn">Yūḥanna ad-Dimashqī</i></span>; <a href="/wiki/Greek_language" title="Greek language">Greek</a>: <span lang="el">Ἰωάννης ὁ Δαμασκηνός</span>, <small><a href="/wiki/Romanization_of_Greek" title="Romanization of Greek">romanized</a>:&#160;</small><span title="Greek-language romanization"><i lang="el-Latn">Ioánnēs ho Damaskēnós</i></span>, <small>IPA:&#160;</small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="el-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Greek" title="Help:IPA/Greek">[ioˈanis o ðamasciˈnos]</a></span>; <a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a>: <i lang="la">Ioannes Damascenus</i>; born <b>Manṣūr ibn Sarjūn</b>, <span title="Arabic-language text"><span lang="ar" dir="rtl">منصور إبن سرجون</span></span>) or <b>John Damascene</b> was a <a href="/wiki/Christian_monasticism#Eastern_Orthodox_tradition" title="Christian monasticism">Christian monk</a>, <a href="/wiki/Priesthood_(Eastern_Orthodox_Church)" title="Priesthood (Eastern Orthodox Church)">priest</a>, <a href="/wiki/Hymnwriter" title="Hymnwriter">hymnographer</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Christian_apologetics" title="Christian apologetics">apologist</a>. Born and raised in <a href="/wiki/Damascus" title="Damascus">Damascus</a> c. 675 or 676; the precise date and place of his death is not known, though tradition places it at his monastery, <a href="/wiki/Mar_Saba" title="Mar Saba">Mar Saba</a>, near <a href="/wiki/Jerusalem" title="Jerusalem">Jerusalem</a> on 4 December 749.<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A <a href="/wiki/Polymath" title="Polymath">polymath</a> whose fields of interest and contribution included <a href="/wiki/Law" title="Law">law</a>, <a href="/wiki/Christian_theology" title="Christian theology">theology</a>, <a href="/wiki/Christian_philosophy" title="Christian philosophy">philosophy</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Music" title="Music">music</a>, he was given the by-name of <b>Chrysorroas</b> (Χρυσορρόας, literally "streaming with gold", i.e. "the golden speaker").
He wrote works expounding the Christian faith, and composed hymns which are still used both <a href="/wiki/Liturgy" title="Liturgy">liturgically</a> in <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Christianity" title="Eastern Christianity">Eastern Christian</a> practice throughout the world as well as in western <a href="/wiki/Lutheranism" title="Lutheranism">Lutheranism</a> at Easter.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He is one of the Fathers of the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox Church</a> and is best known for his strong defence of <a href="/wiki/Icon" title="Icon">icons</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Aquilinap222_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Aquilinap222-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a> regards him as a <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_the_Church" title="Doctor of the Church">Doctor of the Church</a>, often referred to as the <i>Doctor of the Assumption</i> due to his writings on the <a href="/wiki/Assumption_of_Mary" title="Assumption of Mary">Assumption of Mary</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
He was also a prominent exponent of <i><a href="/wiki/Perichoresis" title="Perichoresis">perichoresis</a></i>, and employed the concept as a technical term to describe both the interpenetration of the divine and human natures of Christ and the relationship between the hypostases of the Trinity.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> John is at the end of the <a href="/wiki/Patristic_period" class="mw-redirect" title="Patristic period">Patristic period</a> of dogmatic development, and his contribution less that of theological innovation than that of a summary of the developments of the centuries before him. In <a href="/wiki/Catholic_theology" title="Catholic theology">Catholic theology</a>, he is therefore known as the "last of the Greek Fathers".<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The main source of information for the life of John of Damascus is a work attributed to one John of Jerusalem, identified therein as the <a href="/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Patriarch_of_Jerusalem" title="Greek Orthodox Patriarch of Jerusalem">Patriarch of Jerusalem</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Sahas32_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Sahas32-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> This is an excerpted translation into Greek of an earlier Arabic text. The Arabic original contains a prologue not found in most other translations, and was written by an Arab monk, Michael, who explained that he decided to write his biography in 1084 because none was available in his day. However, the main Arabic text seems to have been written by an earlier author sometime between the early 9th and late 10th century.<sup id="cite_ref-Sahas32_11-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Sahas32-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> 
Written from a hagiographical point of view and prone to exaggeration and some legendary details, it is not the best historical source for his life, but is widely reproduced and considered to contain elements of some value.<sup id="cite_ref-Sahas35_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Sahas35-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> The hagiographic novel <i><a href="/wiki/Barlaam_and_Josaphat" title="Barlaam and Josaphat">Barlaam and Josaphat</a></i>, is a work of the 10th century<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> attributed to a monk named John. It was only considerably later that the tradition arose that this was John of Damascus, but most scholars no longer accept this attribution. Instead much evidence points to <a href="/wiki/Euthymius_of_Athos" class="mw-redirect" title="Euthymius of Athos">Euthymius of Athos</a>, a Georgian who died in 1028.<sup id="cite_ref-loeb_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-loeb-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Family_background">Family background</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_of_Damascus&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Family background">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>John was born in <a href="/wiki/Damascus" title="Damascus">Damascus</a>, in 675 or 676, to a prominent Damascene <a href="/wiki/Arab_Christians" title="Arab Christians">Christian Arab</a> family.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> 
His father, <a href="/wiki/Sarjun_ibn_Mansur" title="Sarjun ibn Mansur">Sarjun ibn Mansur</a>, served as an official of the early <a href="/wiki/Umayyad_Caliphate" title="Umayyad Caliphate">Umayyad Caliphate</a>. His grandfather, <a href="/wiki/Mansur_ibn_Sarjun" title="Mansur ibn Sarjun">Mansur ibn Sarjun</a>, was a prominent <a href="/wiki/Byzantine" class="mw-redirect" title="Byzantine">Byzantine</a> official of Damascus,  who had been responsible for the taxes of the region during the reign of Emperor <a href="/wiki/Heraclius" title="Heraclius">Heraclius</a> and also served under Emperor <a href="/wiki/Maurice_(emperor)" title="Maurice (emperor)">Maurice</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Brownp307_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brownp307-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Janosik25_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Janosik25-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> 
Mansur seems to have played a role in the capitulation of Damascus to the troops of <a href="/wiki/Khalid_ibn_al-Walid" title="Khalid ibn al-Walid">Khalid ibn al-Walid</a> in 635 after securing favorable conditions of surrender.<sup id="cite_ref-Brownp307_17-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brownp307-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Janosik25_18-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Janosik25-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Patriarch_Eutychius_of_Alexandria" class="mw-redirect" title="Patriarch Eutychius of Alexandria">Eutychius</a>, a 10th-century <a href="/wiki/Melkite" title="Melkite">Melkite</a> patriarch, mentions him as one high-ranking official involved in the surrender of the city to the Muslims.<sup id="cite_ref-Sahas17_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Sahas17-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The tribal background of Mansur ibn Sarjun, John's grandfather, is unknown, but biographer Daniel Sahas has speculated that the name Mansur could have implied descent from the <a href="/wiki/Arab_Christians" title="Arab Christians">Arab Christian tribes</a> of <a href="/wiki/Banu_Kalb" title="Banu Kalb">Kalb</a> or <a href="/wiki/Taghlib" title="Taghlib">Taghlib</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Sahas7_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Sahas7-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> 
The name was common among Syrian Christians of Arab origins, and Eutychius noted that the governor of Damascus, who was likely Mansur ibn Sarjun, was an Arab.<sup id="cite_ref-Sahas7_20-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Sahas7-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> However, Sahas also asserts that the name does not necessarily imply an Arab background and could have been used by non-Arab, Semitic Syrians.<sup id="cite_ref-Sahas7_20-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Sahas7-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> While Sahas and biographers F. H. Chase and Andrew Louth assert that <i>Mansūr</i> was an Arabic name, Raymond le Coz asserts that the "family was without doubt of Syrian origin";<sup id="cite_ref-Janosik26_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Janosik26-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> indeed, according to historian Daniel J. Janosik, "Both aspects could be true, for if his family ancestry were indeed Syrian, his grandfather [Mansur] could have been given an Arabic name when the Arabs took over the government."<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> 
When <a href="/wiki/Muslim_conquest_of_the_Levant" title="Muslim conquest of the Levant">Syria was conquered by the Muslim Arabs</a> in the 630s, the court at Damascus retained its large complement of Christian civil servants, John's grandfather among them.<sup id="cite_ref-Brownp307_17-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brownp307-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Sahas17_19-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Sahas17-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> John's father, <a href="/wiki/Sarjun_ibn_Mansur" title="Sarjun ibn Mansur">Sarjun (Sergius)</a>, went on to serve the Umayyad <a href="/wiki/Caliph" class="mw-redirect" title="Caliph">caliphs</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Brownp307_17-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brownp307-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> According to John of Jerusalem and some later versions of his life, after his father's death, John also served as an official to the caliphal court before leaving to become a monk. This claim, that John actually served in a Muslim court, has been questioned since he is never mentioned in Muslim sources, which however do refer to his father Sarjun (Sergius) as a secretary in the caliphal administration.<sup id="cite_ref-Hoyland_1996_481_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hoyland_1996_481-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> In addition, John's own writings never refer to any experience in a Muslim court. It is believed that John became a monk at <a href="/wiki/Mar_Saba" title="Mar Saba">Mar Saba</a>, and that he was ordained as a priest in 735.<sup id="cite_ref-Brownp307_17-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brownp307-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-McEnhillNewlandsp154_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-McEnhillNewlandsp154-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Biography">Biography</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_of_Damascus&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Biography">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Damascus_(arabic_icon).gif" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e0/John_Damascus_%28arabic_icon%29.gif/220px-John_Damascus_%28arabic_icon%29.gif" decoding="async" width="220" height="347" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e0/John_Damascus_%28arabic_icon%29.gif/330px-John_Damascus_%28arabic_icon%29.gif 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/e/e0/John_Damascus_%28arabic_icon%29.gif 2x" data-file-width="364" data-file-height="574" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Damascus_(arabic_icon).gif" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>19th-century icon (Arabic inscription)</div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Nuremberg_chronicles_f_138r_3.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f5/Nuremberg_chronicles_f_138r_3.jpg/220px-Nuremberg_chronicles_f_138r_3.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="216" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/f/f5/Nuremberg_chronicles_f_138r_3.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="234" data-file-height="230" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Nuremberg_chronicles_f_138r_3.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Depiction of John Damascene in the <i><a href="/wiki/Nuremberg_Chronicle" title="Nuremberg Chronicle">Nuremberg Chronicle</a></i></div></div></div>
<p>John was raised in Damascus, and Arab Christian folklore holds that during his adolescence, John associated with the future <a href="/wiki/Umayyad_Caliphate" title="Umayyad Caliphate">Umayyad</a> caliph <a href="/wiki/Yazid_I" title="Yazid I">Yazid I</a> and the Taghlibi Christian court poet <a href="/wiki/Al-Akhtal" class="mw-redirect" title="Al-Akhtal">al-Akhtal</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>One of the <i>vitae</i> describes his father's desire for him to "learn not only the books of the Muslims, but those of the Greeks as well." From this it has been suggested that John may have grown up bilingual.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> John does indeed show some knowledge of the <a href="/wiki/Quran" title="Quran">Quran</a>, which he criticizes harshly.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Other sources describe his education in Damascus as having been conducted in accordance with the principles of <a href="/wiki/Paideia" title="Paideia">Hellenic education</a>, termed "secular" by one source and "classical Christian" by another.<sup id="cite_ref-Louthp284_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Louthp284-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Butlerp36_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Butlerp36-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> One account identifies his tutor as a monk by the name of <a href="/wiki/Cosmas_the_Monk" title="Cosmas the Monk">Cosmas</a>, who had been kidnapped by Arabs from his home in <a href="/wiki/Sicily" title="Sicily">Sicily</a>, and for whom John's father paid a great price.
</p><p>Under the instruction of Cosmas, who also taught John's orphan friend, <a href="/wiki/Cosmas_of_Maiuma" title="Cosmas of Maiuma">Cosmas of Maiuma</a>, John is said to have made great advances<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="margin-left:0.1em; white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Please_clarify" title="Wikipedia:Please clarify"><span title="The text near this tag may need clarification or removal of jargon. (March 2021)">clarification needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> in music, astronomy and theology, soon rivalling <a href="/wiki/Pythagoras" title="Pythagoras">Pythagoras</a> in arithmetic and <a href="/wiki/Euclid" title="Euclid">Euclid</a> in <a href="/wiki/Geometry" title="Geometry">geometry</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Butlerp36_29-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Butlerp36-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> 
As a refugee from Italy, Cosmas brought with him the scholarly traditions of <a href="/wiki/Latin_Christianity" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin Christianity">Latin Christianity</a>.
</p><p>John   possibly had a career as a civil servant for the <a href="/wiki/Umayyad_Caliphate" title="Umayyad Caliphate">Caliph</a> in Damascus before his ordination.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He then became a priest and monk at the <a href="/wiki/Mar_Saba" title="Mar Saba">Mar Saba</a> monastery near Jerusalem. 
One source suggests John left Damascus to become a monk around 706, when <a href="/wiki/Al-Walid_I" title="Al-Walid I">al-Walid I</a> increased the Islamicisation of the Caliphate's administration.<sup id="cite_ref-Louthp9_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Louthp9-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> 
This is uncertain, as Muslim sources only mention that his father Sarjun (Sergius) left the administration around this time, and fail to name John at all.<sup id="cite_ref-Hoyland_1996_481_23-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hoyland_1996_481-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> 
During the next two decades, culminating in the <a href="/wiki/Siege_of_Constantinople_(717-718)" class="mw-redirect" title="Siege of Constantinople (717-718)">Siege of Constantinople (717-718)</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Umayyad_Caliphate" title="Umayyad Caliphate">Umayyad Caliphate</a> progressively occupied the borderlands of the Byzantine Empire. An editor of John's works, Father <a href="/wiki/Michel_Le_Quien" title="Michel Le Quien">Le Quien</a>, has shown that John was already a monk at Mar Saba before the dispute over iconoclasm, explained below.<sup id="cite_ref-catholic_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-catholic-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the early 8th century, <a href="/wiki/Iconoclasm_(Byzantine)" class="mw-redirect" title="Iconoclasm (Byzantine)">iconoclasm</a>, a movement opposed to the veneration of icons, gained acceptance in the <a href="/wiki/Byzantine" class="mw-redirect" title="Byzantine">Byzantine</a> court. In 726, despite the protests of <a href="/wiki/Patriarch_Germanus_I_of_Constantinople" class="mw-redirect" title="Patriarch Germanus I of Constantinople">Germanus</a>, <a href="/wiki/List_of_Ecumenical_Patriarchs_of_Constantinople" class="mw-redirect" title="List of Ecumenical Patriarchs of Constantinople">Patriarch of Constantinople</a>, Emperor <a href="/wiki/Leo_III_the_Isaurian" title="Leo III the Isaurian">Leo III</a> (who had forced his predecessor, <a href="/wiki/Theodosius_III" title="Theodosius III">Theodosius III</a>, to abdicate and himself assumed the throne in 717 immediately before the great siege) issued his first edict against the <a href="/wiki/Veneration" title="Veneration">veneration</a> of images and their exhibition in public places.<sup id="cite_ref-cathen_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cathen-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>All agree that John of Damascus undertook a spirited defence of holy images in three separate publications. The earliest of these works, his <i>Apologetic Treatises against those Decrying the Holy Images</i>, secured his reputation. He not only attacked the Byzantine emperor, but adopted a simplified style that allowed the controversy to be followed by the common people, stirring rebellion among the iconoclasts. Decades after his death, John's writings would play an important role during the <a href="/wiki/Second_Council_of_Nicaea" title="Second Council of Nicaea">Second Council of Nicaea</a> (787), which convened to settle the icon dispute.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>John's biography recounts at least one episode deemed improbable or legendary.<sup id="cite_ref-catholic_32-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-catholic-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Jamesonp24_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Jamesonp24-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> Leo III reportedly sent forged documents to the caliph which implicated John in a plot to attack <a href="/wiki/Damascus" title="Damascus">Damascus</a>. The caliph then ordered John's right hand be cut off and hung up in public view. Some days afterwards, John asked for the restitution of his hand, and prayed fervently to the <i><a href="/wiki/Theotokos" title="Theotokos">Theotokos</a></i> before her icon: thereupon, his hand is said to have been miraculously restored.<sup id="cite_ref-catholic_32-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-catholic-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> In gratitude for this miraculous healing, he attached a silver hand to the icon, which thereafter became known as the "Three-handed", or <a href="/wiki/Trojeru%C4%8Dica" title="Trojeručica"><i>Tricheirousa</i></a>.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> That icon is now located in the <a href="/wiki/Hilandar" title="Hilandar">Hilandar</a> monastery of the <a href="/wiki/Mount_Athos" title="Mount Athos">Holy Mountain</a>.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Veneration">Veneration</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_of_Damascus&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Veneration">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>When the name of John of Damascus was inserted in the <a href="/wiki/General_Roman_Calendar" title="General Roman Calendar">General Roman Calendar</a> in 1890, it was assigned to 27 March. The feast day was moved in 1969 to the day of John's death, 4 December, the day on which his feast day is celebrated also in the <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_Rite" title="Byzantine Rite">Byzantine Rite</a> calendar,<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> Lutheran Commemorations,<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> and the Anglican Communion and Episcopal Church.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>John of Damascus is honored in the <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Church_of_England)" title="Calendar of saints (Church of England)">Church of England</a> and in the <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Episcopal_Church)" title="Calendar of saints (Episcopal Church)">Episcopal Church</a> on 4 December.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1890, he was declared a <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_the_Church" title="Doctor of the Church">Doctor of the Church</a> by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Leo_XIII" title="Pope Leo XIII">Pope Leo XIII</a>.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="List_of_works">List of works</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_of_Damascus&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: List of works">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Ioannis_Damasceni_Opera.tif" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0e/Ioannis_Damasceni_Opera.tif/lossy-page1-170px-Ioannis_Damasceni_Opera.tif.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="289" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0e/Ioannis_Damasceni_Opera.tif/lossy-page1-255px-Ioannis_Damasceni_Opera.tif.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0e/Ioannis_Damasceni_Opera.tif/lossy-page1-340px-Ioannis_Damasceni_Opera.tif.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3820" data-file-height="6485" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Ioannis_Damasceni_Opera.tif" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><i>Ioannis Damasceni Opera</i> (1603)</div></div></div>
<p>Besides his purely textual works, many of which are listed below, John of Damascus also composed hymns, perfecting the <a href="/wiki/Canon_(hymnography)" title="Canon (hymnography)">canon</a>, a structured hymn form used in <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_Rite" title="Byzantine Rite">Byzantine Rite</a> liturgies.<sup id="cite_ref-Shahidp195_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Shahidp195-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_works">Early works</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_of_Damascus&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Early works">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<ul><li>Three <i><a href="/w/index.php?title=Apologetic_Treatises_against_those_Decrying_the_Holy_Images&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Apologetic Treatises against those Decrying the Holy Images (page does not exist)">Apologetic Treatises against those Decrying the Holy Images</a></i> – These treatises were among his earliest expositions in response to the edict by the Byzantine Emperor <a href="/wiki/Leo_III_the_Isaurian" title="Leo III the Isaurian">Leo III</a>, banning the veneration or exhibition of holy images.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Teachings_and_dogmatic_works">Teachings and dogmatic works</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_of_Damascus&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Teachings and dogmatic works">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<ul><li><i><a href="/w/index.php?title=Fountain_of_Knowledge&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Fountain of Knowledge (page does not exist)">Fountain of Knowledge</a></i> or <i>The Fountain of Wisdom</i>, is divided into three parts:
<ol><li><i>Philosophical Chapters</i> (<i>Kefálea filosofiká</i>) – commonly called "Dialectic", it deals mostly with logic, its primary purpose being to prepare the reader for a better understanding of the rest of the book.</li>
<li><i>Concerning Heresy</i> (<i>Perì eréseon</i>) – the last chapter of this part (Chapter 101) deals with the <i>Heresy of the Ishmaelites</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> Unlike earlier sections devoted to other heresies, which are disposed of succinctly in just a few lines, this chapter runs into several pages. It constitutes one of the first Christian refutations of <a href="/wiki/Islam" title="Islam">Islam</a>. One of the most dominant translations is his polemical work <i>Peri haireseon</i> translated from Greek into Latin. His manuscript is one of the first Orthodox Christian refutations of Islam which has influenced the Western Catholic Church's attitude. It was among the first sources representing the Prophet of Islam <a href="/wiki/Muhammad" title="Muhammad">Muhammad</a> to the West as "false prophet," and "Antichrist."<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><i><a href="/w/index.php?title=An_Exact_Exposition_of_the_Orthodox_Faith&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="An Exact Exposition of the Orthodox Faith (page does not exist)">An Exact Exposition of the Orthodox Faith</a></i> (<i>Ékdosis akribès tēs Orthodóxou Písteōs</i>) – a summary of the dogmatic writings of the <a href="/wiki/Early_Church_Fathers" class="mw-redirect" title="Early Church Fathers">Early Church Fathers</a>. This writing was the first work of <a href="/wiki/Systematic_theology" title="Systematic theology">systematic theology</a> in <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Christianity" title="Eastern Christianity">Eastern Christianity</a> and an important influence on later Scholastic works.<sup id="cite_ref-retrome_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-retrome-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup></li></ol></li>
<li><i>Against the Jacobites</i></li>
<li><i>Against the Nestorians</i></li>
<li><i>Dialogue against the Manichees</i></li>
<li><i>Elementary Introduction into Dogmas</i></li>
<li><i>Letter on the Thrice-Holy Hymn</i></li>
<li><i>On Right Thinking</i></li>
<li><i>On the Faith, Against the Nestorians</i></li>
<li><i>On the Two Wills in Christ (Against the <a href="/wiki/Monothelite" class="mw-redirect" title="Monothelite">Monothelites</a>)</i></li>
<li><i>Sacred Parallels</i> (dubious)</li>
<li><i><a href="/wiki/Octoechos_(liturgy)" title="Octoechos (liturgy)">Octoechos</a></i> (the church's liturgical book of eight tones)</li>
<li><i>On Dragons and Ghosts</i></li></ul>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Teaching_on_Islam">Teaching on Islam</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_of_Damascus&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Teaching on Islam">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>As stated above, in the final chapter of <i>Concerning Heresy,</i> John mentions Islam as <i>the Heresy of the Ishmaelites</i>. He is one of the first known Christian critics of Islam. John claims that Muslims were once worshipers of Aphrodite who followed after Mohammad because of his "seeming show of piety," and that Mohammad himself read the Bible and, "likewise, it seems," spoke to an Arian monk that taught him Arianism instead of Christianity. John also claims to have read the Quran, or at least parts of it, as he criticizes the Quran for saying that the Virgin Mary was the sister of Moses and Aaron and that Jesus was not crucified but brought alive into heaven. John further claims to have spoken to Muslims about Mohammad. He uses the <a href="/wiki/Nosism" title="Nosism">plural "we"</a>, whether in reference to himself, or to a group of Christians that he belonged to who spoke to the Muslims, or in reference to Christians in general.<sup id="cite_ref-orthodoxinfo.com_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-orthodoxinfo.com-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Regardless, John claims that he asked the Muslims what witnesses can testify that Mohammad received the Quran from God – since, John says, Moses received the Torah from God in the presence of the Israelites, and since Islamic law mandates that a Muslim can only marry and do trade in the presence of witnesses – and what biblical prophets and verses foretold Mohammad's coming – since, John says, Jesus was foretold by the prophets and whole Old Testament. John claims that the Muslims answered that Mohammad received the Quran in his sleep. John claims that he jokingly answered, "You're spinning my dreams."<sup id="cite_ref-orthodoxinfo.com_47-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-orthodoxinfo.com-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Some of the Muslims, John says, claimed that the Old Testament that Christians believe foretells Jesus' coming is misinterpreted, while other Muslims claimed that the Jews edited the Old Testament so as to deceive Christians (possibly into believing Jesus is God, but John does not say).<sup id="cite_ref-orthodoxinfo.com_47-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-orthodoxinfo.com-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>While recounting his alleged dialogue with Muslims, John claims that they have accused him of idol worship for venerating the Cross and worshipping Jesus. John claims that he told the Muslims that the black stone in Mecca was the head of a statue of Aphrodite. Moreover, he claims, the Muslims were wrong not to associate Jesus with God if Jesus is the Word of God. John claims that, if Jesus is the Word of God, and the Word of God has always existed with God, then the Word must be a part of God, and therefore be God himself, whereby, John says, it would be wrong for Muslims to call Jesus the Word of God but not God himself.<sup id="cite_ref-orthodoxinfo.com_47-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-orthodoxinfo.com-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>John ends the chapter quickly by claiming that Islam permits <a href="/wiki/Polygamy" title="Polygamy">polygamy</a>, that Mohammad committed adultery with a companion's wife before outlawing adultery, and that the Quran is filled with ridiculous stories, such as the <a href="/wiki/She-Camel_of_God" class="mw-redirect" title="She-Camel of God">She-Camel of God</a> and God giving Jesus an "incorruptible table."<sup id="cite_ref-orthodoxinfo.com_47-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-orthodoxinfo.com-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Arabic_translation">Arabic translation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_of_Damascus&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Arabic translation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:John_of_Damascus.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/John_of_Damascus.jpg/170px-John_of_Damascus.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="247" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/John_of_Damascus.jpg/255px-John_of_Damascus.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/John_of_Damascus.jpg/340px-John_of_Damascus.jpg 2x" data-file-width="350" data-file-height="508" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:John_of_Damascus.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Icon by Michael Anagnostou Chomatzas  (1734)</div></div></div>
<p>It is believed that the homily on the Annunciation was the first work to be translated into Arabic. Much of this text is found in Manuscript 4226 of the Library of Strasbourg (France), dating to 885 AD.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Later in the 10th century, Antony, superior of the monastery of St. Simon (near Antioch) translated a corpus of John Damascene. In his introduction to John's work, Sylvestre patriarch of Antioch (1724–1766) said that Antony was monk at Saint Saba. This could be a misunderstanding of the title Superior of Saint Simon probably because Saint Simon's monastery was in ruins in the 18th century.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Most manuscripts give the text of the letter to Cosmas,<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> the philosophical chapters,<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> the theological chapters and five other small works.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1085, Mikhael, a monk from Antioch, wrote the Arabic life of the Chrysorrhoas.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> This work was first edited by Bacha in 1912 and then translated into many languages (German, Russian and English).
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Modern_English_translations">Modern English translations</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_of_Damascus&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Modern English translations">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<ul><li><i>On holy images; followed by three sermons on the Assumption</i>, translated by Mary H. Allies, (London: Thomas Baker, 1898)</li>
<li><i>Exposition of the Orthodox faith</i>, translated by the Reverend SDF Salmond, in Select Library of Nicene and Post-Nicene Fathers. 2nd Series vol 9. (Oxford: Parker, 1899) [reprint Grand Rapids, MI: Eerdmans, 1963.]</li>
<li><i>Writings</i>, translated by Frederic H. Chase. Fathers of the Church vol 37, (Washington, DC: Catholic University of America Press, 1958) [ET of <i>The fount of knowledge</i>; <i>On heresies</i>; <i>The orthodox faith</i>]</li>
<li>Daniel J. Sahas (ed.), <i>John of Damascus on Islam: The "Heresy of the Ishmaelites"</i>, (Leiden: Brill, 1972)</li>
<li><i>On the divine images: the apologies against those who attack the divine images</i>, translated by David Anderson, (New York: St. Vladimir's Seminary Press, 1980)</li>
<li><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1133582631">.mw-parser-output cite.citation{font-style:inherit;word-wrap:break-word}.mw-parser-output .citation q{quotes:"\"""\"""'""'"}.mw-parser-output .citation:target{background-color:rgba(0,127,255,0.133)}.mw-parser-output .id-lock-free a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-free a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/6/65/Lock-green.svg")right 0.1em center/9px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output .id-lock-limited a,.mw-parser-output .id-lock-registration a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-limited a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-registration a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/d/d6/Lock-gray-alt-2.svg")right 0.1em center/9px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output .id-lock-subscription a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-subscription a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/a/aa/Lock-red-alt-2.svg")right 0.1em center/9px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output .cs1-ws-icon a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/4/4c/Wikisource-logo.svg")right 0.1em center/12px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output .cs1-code{color:inherit;background:inherit;border:none;padding:inherit}.mw-parser-output .cs1-hidden-error{display:none;color:#d33}.mw-parser-output .cs1-visible-error{color:#d33}.mw-parser-output .cs1-maint{display:none;color:#3a3;margin-left:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .cs1-format{font-size:95%}.mw-parser-output .cs1-kern-left{padding-left:0.2em}.mw-parser-output .cs1-kern-right{padding-right:0.2em}.mw-parser-output .citation .mw-selflink{font-weight:inherit}</style><cite class="citation book cs1"><i>Three Treatises on the Divine Images</i>. <p>Two translations exist of the 10th-century hagiographic novel <i><a href="/wiki/Barlaam_and_Josaphat" title="Barlaam and Josaphat">Barlaam and Josaphat</a></i>, traditionally attributed to John:
</p>
<ul><li><i>Barlaam and Ioasaph</i>, with an English translation by G.R. Woodward and H. Mattingly, (London: Heinemann, 1914)</li>
<li><i>The precious pearl: the lives of Saints Barlaam and Ioasaph, </i>notes and comments by Augoustinos N Kantiotes; preface, introduction, and new translation by Asterios Gerostergios, et al., (Belmont, MA: Institute for Byzantine and Modern Greek Studies, 1997)</li></ul>

<li id="cite_note-10"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-10">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">
O'Connor, J.B. (1910) "John of Damascus was the last of the Greek Fathers. His genius was not for original theological development, but for compilation of an encyclopedic character. In fact, the state of full development to which theological thought had been brought by the great Greek writers and councils left him little else than the work of an encyclopedist; and this work he performed in such manner as to merit the gratitude of all succeeding ages".
In Orthodox Christianity, the concept of "fathers of the Church" is used somewhat more loosely, with no exhaustive list or end date, with a <a href="/wiki/List_of_Church_Fathers#After_John_of_Damascus" title="List of Church Fathers">number of theologians</a> younger than John Damascene generally included.</span>
